Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are E (black) and J (black). I list E’s neighbors: F, L, M, B. J’s neighbors: M, I, P, D. There is no direct E–J edge.
- Any E→J path must start from one of {F, L, M, B} and end entering J via one of {M, I, P, D}.

Check possible routes:
1) Through M: E–M–J is a direct length-2 path. To break this, we must remove M (since removing E or J is forbidden).
2) Through other neighbors if M is removed:
   - From E via F: F connects to G, K, X and to the rest of the graph via G→H→B→… So paths like E–F–G–H–I–J or E–F–G–H–B–C–I–J, etc., exist.
   - From E via B: E–B–C–I–J or E–B–H–I–J are possible.
   - From E via L: L only connects to M and E, so with M removed, L is a dead end and offers no route.

Thus after removing M, remaining E→J paths funnel through I or P (since D connects only to P and J: D–P–J). Indeed:
- From E via B or F, we can reach I (e.g., E–B–H–I or E–F–G–H–I) and then I–J.
- Or reach P via O/N/C then P–J (e.g., E–B–C–O–P–J, or E–F–G–H–B–C–O–P–J).
Therefore, with M removed, every surviving path to J must pass through either I or P to enter J (since D attaches to J only through P).

Minimum cut:
- Remove M to kill the shortest E–M–J path and disconnect L.
- Additionally remove either I or P to block all alternative entries to J.
- A single-node cut is impossible because M alone doesn’t stop routes through I/P, and removing only I or only P leaves E–M–J intact.

Verify minimality:
- Any cut must block the E–M–J path, forcing inclusion of M.
- After removing M, at least one of {I, P} must be removed to stop all remaining paths. Hence size 2 is minimal.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, I] or [M, P]
--- Ground Truth ---

Image name: Tu_task2_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

Identify the black target nodes: H and E.

List connections near H:
- H connects to C, I, and G.

List connections near E:
- E connects to B, P, F, L, M.

Trace all possible paths from H to E:
1) Through C/B side:
   - H–C–B–E
   - H–C–B–P–E
   - H–I–B–E (since I–B edge exists via the central cluster)
   - Variants via N/O/Y but all funnel into B or P before reaching E.

2) Through I/P/J side:
   - H–I–P–E
   - H–I–J–P–E
   - H–I–J–E (no direct J–E edge; J connects to P and D, so still goes via P)
   - H–I–P–J–… (still must pass P to reach E)

3) Through G/F side:
   - H–G–F–E
   - H–G–F–P–E
   - H–G–F–(K/X triangle)–F–… ultimately must return to F, then to E or P.

Observation: Every H–E path must pass through at least one of the three nodes {B, P, F} just before reaching E (since E’s only neighbors that connect to the broader graph are B, P, and F; L and M are leaves attached to E only).

Therefore, {B, P, F} is a vertex cut separating H from E. We need to check minimality:

- If we remove only one node:
  • Remove B: Path H–G–F–E or H–I–P–E remains.
  • Remove P: Path H–C–B–E or H–G–F–E remains.
  • Remove F: Path H–C–B–E or H–I–P–E remains.
  So any single removal fails.

- If we remove any two of them:
  • Remove {B, P}: Path via F remains (H–G–F–E).
  • Remove {B, F}: Path via P remains (H–I–P–E).
  • Remove {P, F}: Path via B remains (H–C–B–E).
  Hence two nodes are insufficient.

Thus the minimum is three nodes, and removing all three {B, P, F} breaks every path from H to E. No smaller cut exists given the above checks.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, P, F]

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are H (left-middle) and E (upper-middle).
- List connections:
  - E connects to F, L, M, and B.
  - H connects to G, B, and I (via the slanted edge from H to I).
- Any path from H to E must enter E via one of {F, L, M, B}. From H’s side, all routes go through either B, I→J/M, or G→F→E.
- Check bottlenecks:
  1) Paths via B: H–B–E is direct through B; also H–B–C–…–I–…–J–M–E etc. Removing B blocks every route that uses B.
  2) Paths avoiding B:
     - H–I–J–M–E
     - H–I–P–…–J–M–E
     - H–G–F–E
     - H–G–F–(others)–…–E (but any return to E from that side reaches E through F)
     - H–I–… cannot reach E except through J→M or looping back to B, F, or L.
     - L connects only to E and M, so any use of L must pass through M.
  Thus, aside from B, the remaining independent gateway to E is through F or through M (J→M). If we remove F and M, all B-avoiding paths are cut.
- Verify sufficiency: Remove nodes {B, F}?
  - H–G–F–E is cut.
  - H–I–J–M–E still exists, so need M (or J/I). 
- Minimality: 
  - Removing {B, M}:
    - Direct H–B–E cut.
    - H–I–J–M–E cut by removing M.
    - H–G–F–E still possible via F. So {B, M} is not enough.
  - Removing {B, F} not enough (path via J–M).
  - Removing {F, M} without B leaves H–B–E path, so not enough.
  Therefore, we need three nodes to hit all independent entrances to E from H: B (blocks B-route), F (blocks G/F-route), and M (blocks I/J-route and any L-mediated route).

- H–I–J–M–E
By Menger’s theorem, minimum cut size is at least 3, and {B, F, M} achieves it.

There is a direct edge L–E, so to stop entry via L we must cut (L, E) specifically. Therefore, we must cut both (M, E) and (L, E) unless some other cut prevents reaching L entirely, which isn’t guaranteed due to the outer frame.

Hence, to guarantee disconnection regardless of alternate routes, remove all edges into E: (E, B), (E, F), (E, M), (E, L). After removing these four edges, E is isolated; no path from H can reach E.

Minimality check:
- With any 3-edge removal leaving one of E’s incident edges, there exists a path from H to that neighbor (H–B, H–G–F, or H–I–…–M/L) and then directly into E. So 3 edges are insufficient.
- Therefore 4 is the minimum.

     c) G–H–I–P–O–N (via I/P)
     d) G–F–X–K–J–P–O–N or variants reaching P then O.
     e) G–F–X–C–N (since X–C? No direct line; only X–F, X–K, X–G. So X doesn’t connect to C.)
     f) G–F–(to K)–J–P–O–N (F–K is present)
     g) G–E–B–C–N (E–B, B–C are present)
     h) G–E–B–O–N (B–O via C? There is a B–C edge and C–O; there is also B–O direct? The diagram shows B–C horizontal, C–O diagonal, and a B–O horizontal? No, B has a horizontal to C only; O is right of C. So path is B–C–O–N.)

3) Through the right/top side:
   - G–F–K–J–(P or down to D)–… to O–N
   - G–X–K–J–P–O–N
   - G–H–I–J–P–O–N (I–J diagonal is present)
   - Also J connects to D and to P and to M. D only connects to J; dead end for reaching N unless coming back to P→O.
   - M connects to J and to F and to E (M–E? The top-left slant M–E line exists). From G using E→M→J still funnels to J/P/O to reach N.

Observation: To reach N from the right/top, all routes funnel through the pair {P, O} before N:
- The only neighbors of N are {B, C, O, Y}. From the right/top side, the only entry among these is O (Y sits below O and connects to N and O, but to reach Y you must pass through O via edge Y–O). So any route coming from J/P side must use edge into O (via P–O) and then to N (either O–N directly or O–C–N). Therefore, cutting O alone would sever every path that tries to come via J/P/right side and also break the middle-bottom C–O–N variants.

Now check if removing O suffices:
- Remaining possible G→N paths without O:
  Left column: G–E–B–N and G–H–B–N and G–H–C–N and G–E–B–C–N (C–N is an edge).
  So O alone is not enough because C–N edge allows bypass.

We need to block the left/middle direct connections to N: neighbors B, C, Y (Y also connects to N and O; without O, Y still connects to N and to C? Y connects to N and to O only per drawing; plus to C? There is a C–Y diagonal? The drawing shows C has lines to B, O, and N; Y connects to N and O only. So if O is removed, Y is isolated from the rest and cannot help reach N.)

So with O removed, the only remaining neighbors of N that could be reached are B and C. To stop those, we could remove B or C (one of them is enough if O is gone? We must ensure both B and C routes are blocked.)
- If we remove O and B, paths via C remain: G–H–C–N or G–E–B–C–N fails because B removed, but G–H–C–N remains. So need C instead of B.
- If we remove O and C, left via B still has G–E–B–N and G–H–B–N. So C alone with O is not enough unless we also block B.

Therefore, a size-2 cut could be {O, B} and also block C–based paths? Wait above shows {O, B} leaves G–H–C–N intact. So not sufficient.

Try targeting N’s neighbors directly: To disconnect G from N, it suffices to delete all nodes in a separator that intercepts every approach to the neighbor set {B, C, O, Y}. Minimal try:
- Remove two nodes E and H? If we remove E and H, can G still reach the lower area? From G via F/X/K/J/P to O then N exists; but if we also remove O, that's third node. So E+H alone not enough.
- Remove B and C: Then only neighbors {O, Y} remain. From G to O exists via many routes; so need also O or P. So not enough.

Consider a 2-node cut: Is there a single articulation node whose removal funnels all paths with another? Candidates: at the right, P seems crucial since all right/top routes must go through P to get to O. But left routes through B or C do not use P. So P plus one of {E,H,B,C} maybe? If remove P, the only remaining connections to reach N are via left/middle through B or C or O via C (but P gone removes access to O from right; O can still be reached from C via C–O. So to kill O access altogether we would need to remove C or O along with P. But if we remove P and C, left route via B still exists (G–E/H–B–N). If we remove P and B, path via G–H–C–N exists. So any 2-node set among {P} plus one of B/C fails.

Try 2-node set {B, C}: With B and C removed, neighbors of N left: O and Y. Can G reach O or Y? G can reach O via right side J–P–O. So {B, C} fails.

Try {B, O}: We saw fails due to C–N.

Try {C, O}: Fails due to B–N.

Try {B, Y}: Y is irrelevant; {B, Y} leaves C–N and also P–O–N.

Thus 2-node cut seems impossible.

Try 3-node cut. Natural is to delete all neighbors of N: {B, C, O, Y}. That certainly disconnects, but maybe not minimal. Can we do it with 3 nodes? We need to block both left (to B or C) and right (to O). Consider removing B, C, and O: That leaves only neighbor Y to N. But Y only connects to O and N; with O removed, Y becomes a leaf to N with no connection to G. Therefore {B, C, O} disconnects. Can any 3-node combination work without removing all three of these? Maybe remove O and also block access to both B and C upstream with a single node? Is there a single node whose removal blocks both G→B and G→C routes? G→B paths: G–E–B and G–H–B. G→C paths: G–H–C and G–E–B–C. To block both without deleting B or C, removing E and H together would block both; that’s two nodes. Pair with O makes 3: {E, H, O}. Check: With E and H removed, G has no path to B or C. Could G still reach N via O? Yes unless we remove O. With O removed too, no path remains (Y needs O). So {E, H, O} is a valid 3-cut.

Can we do 2 by choosing a different choke earlier? What about removing E and B only, plus rely on something else? Paths via H–C–N remain. So no.

Is there a single node that if removed blocks left completely? Removing B does not, since H–C–N remains. Removing C does not, since B–N remains. Removing H alone leaves E–B–N; removing E alone leaves H–B–N. So left cannot be blocked by one node.

Therefore minimum is 3. We can present a minimal cut set such as {B, C, O} or {E, H, O}. Verify:

- Remove {B, C, O}.
  Remaining neighbors of N: only Y. Y connects only to O and N; with O removed, Y is isolated from rest. Thus no path from G to N.
  Check necessity: Any 2-node subset fails as shown earlier because at least one of B or C or O would remain, creating a path: 
  - If O remains, right-side route G→…→P→O→N exists.
  - If O is removed but B remains, left route via B exists.
  - If O is removed but C remains, left route via C exists.
  Hence size 2 is impossible.

   - Because H–B–N is a 2-edge path sharing only the middle vertex B, any cut of size <3 must remove at least one of (H,B) or (B,N).
   - If we remove (H,B) only, paths through I or G to N still exist (e.g., H–I–P–O–N and H–G–B–N via (B,N)).
   - If we remove (B,N) only, H–B connects to N via B–C–N or B–C–O–N, etc.
   - Try two-edge cuts:
     a) Remove (H,B) and (H,I): path H–G–B–N remains.
     b) Remove (H,B) and (H,G): path H–I–P–O–N remains.
     c) Remove (H,I) and (H,G): path H–B–N remains.
     d) Any combination that doesn’t touch two of H’s incident edges requires cutting multiple downstream connections from both G-side and I/B-side to N, but because there are parallel routes (via B and via I→P→O→N), at least one survives.
Hence, any 2-edge set fails; 3 edges are necessary and sufficient.

Therefore, the minimal cut is to remove all edges incident to H.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']]}

  - H connects to I and G; via I→J→(edge to M’s side through P? no) but there is also H→I→J and J connects to M? J is connected to the long edge from M via J—actually J is not directly to M; however E–M path already exists, and H also connects to G→F→M and to I→…→P→(edge from P up to M via the long M–P edge). Hence H can reach M.
  - C connects up (C→I→J) and to P; P connects directly to M. So C can reach M.
  - N connects along the base to O and via the right side up to P, and P connects to M. So N can reach M.

Thus every B–M path must pass through at least one of {E, H, C, N} immediately after B.

- Can we disconnect by removing fewer than 2 nodes?
  - Removing any single node among {E, H, C, N} still leaves other neighbors that have routes to M (e.g., remove E, path B→C→P→M remains; remove C, path B→E→M remains; remove H, path B→E→M remains; remove N, path B→C→P→M remains). So 1 node is insufficient.

- Are two nodes enough? We need to choose nodes so that all four exits are blocked or made unable to reach M.
  - If we remove E and P, then:
    - Exit via E is cut.
    - Exit via C relies on C→P (or C→I→J→P) to get to M; with P removed, no route from that side to M.
    - Exit via N relies on the right side reaching P and then M; removing P blocks this.
    - Exit via H: H→G/F side would need F→M, but F connects directly to M; however removing E does not remove F. Check H→G→F→M: this would still exist, so we must ensure F is not reachable. But H connects to G, G connects to F, and F connects to M. Therefore [E, P] is not sufficient.

  - Try removing F and P:
    - Exit via E: E→M requires F? No, E also has a direct edge to M. But with F removed, E still connects directly to M, so [F, P] is not sufficient because B→E→M still works.
  
  - Try removing E and F:
    - Exit via E is gone.
    - Exit via H: H→G→F→M broken because F removed. H→I→J→…→P→M still possible unless P is cut; but P remains, so path B→H→I→P→M exists. Not sufficient.
  
  - Try removing E and J:
    - E path broken.
    - C path: C→P→M still works (doesn’t need J). Not sufficient.
  
  - Try removing E and C:
    - E path broken.
    - C path broken.
    - N path: N→…→P→M still works.
    - H path: H→I→P→M or H→G→F→M still works. Not sufficient.

  - Observe: to block H’s two alternatives, we must remove either F (blocks H→G→F→M) and P or I/J (to block H→I→…→M via P or M). Since P connects directly to M and also collects right-side routes (from C and N), removing P simultaneously blocks exits C and N and the H→I branch.
    - Then we also must block the E branch separately.
    - But with P removed, H could still go via G→F→M. So alongside P we must also block either F or E (because B could also go to E). If we remove P and F:
      - B→E→M still exists (since E has direct edge to M independent of F). So we must remove P and E together, and also ensure H cannot go via G→F→M; but with E present removed and F present? If we choose [P, E], earlier we saw H→G→F→M remains, which bypasses P and E. So [P, E] fails.
    - To neutralize H completely with P removed, we must remove F (not E). But then we also need to handle E’s direct edge. That suggests [P, F] plus something to stop E—meaning size 3.

Therefore the minimal cut appears to be 3:
- Choose [P, F, E]:
  - E path: removed (E gone).
  - C path: needs P; P removed.
  - N path: goes through right side to P then to M; P removed.
  - H path: H→I→… requires P; blocked. H→G→F→M requires F; blocked.
  - No other neighbor provides a path from B to M.

- Can a different size-2 set work? To succeed with 2 nodes, we’d need to simultaneously block:
  - E’s direct route, and
  - All routes through {C, N, H}.
  Removing P blocks C and N and the H→I branch, but H still has G→F→M unless F is also removed; however with [P, F], E still provides a direct route. Any pair that includes E and F still leaves C/N via P. Any pair including E and something other than P leaves either C or N open. Hence no 2-node cut exists.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [E, F, P]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'E']}

  4) Y → N → B → E → M → J

    a) Through O/P to J: N → O → P → J.
    b) Through B/C/I or B/E/M to J: N → (B → C → I → J) or (B → E → M → J) or (B → H → I → J).

    - With I removed: all routes via C/H/B toward J are broken (N→B→H→I→J; N→B→C→I→J). The alternative E→M→J still remains.
    - With O removed as well: the O/P branch is cut (N→O→P→J impossible). The remaining possible path N→B→E→M→J gets to M→J, but J is still connected to M; since we did not remove M, we must ensure this is blocked: note that with I removed, B→E→M→J still exists. Thus we need to block M rather than O, or block P instead of O and also block M or E.
  - Better pair: {I, M}.
    - Remove I: blocks all C/H/B routes to J.
    - Remove M: blocks the E/M route to J.
    - With both I and M removed, there is no remaining way to reach J except via O→P→J. But that requires passing through I? No; N→O→P→J would still exist. So we must also block that branch. Hence {I, M} alone is insufficient.

- Therefore we need to cut three gateways to cover all branches: one for the O/P branch, one for the I branch, and one for the M branch. Minimal set of size 3:
  - {I, M, O} works:
    - I removed: kills all paths via C/H/B to J.

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

Targets are H (top center-left) and N (bottom center). List all edges incident to H: (H,G), (H,I), (H,B). Any H–N path must leave H via one of these three edges.

From each neighbor, enumerate routes to N:
- From G: G–F–E–B–(to C)–N or G–H–I–C–N (but using H again loops back). So via G the only viable route goes G–F–E–B–C–N (or B–O–N).
- From I: I–C–N or I–P–O–N or I–B–C–N (etc.). All go through either C or O just above N.
- From B: B–C–N or B–O–N.

Therefore, every H–N path, regardless of which neighbor of H it starts with, must pass through at least one of the two edges incident to N’s immediate neighbors: (C, N) or (O, N). Cutting both (C, N) and (O, N) isolates N from the rest of the graph while keeping nodes intact.

Verification:
- After removing (C, N) and (O, N), N has no remaining connections to the graph, so no path from H (or any node) to N exists.
- Removing only one of them leaves a path via the other neighbor (e.g., H–I–C–N if only (O, N) is cut; or H–I–P–O–N if only (C, N) is cut). Thus, two edges are necessary and sufficient.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(C, N), (O, N)]

  - I connects to C, H, P, J (no K).

- Check for K:

  The shortest concrete path:
    N → C (edge exists)
    C → I (edge exists)
    I → H (edge exists)
    H → G (edge exists)
    G → K (edge exists)

This path length is 5 edges. Verify no shorter:
- From Level 3 we had nodes {E, H, J, F}; none connects directly to K.
- From Level 2 {B, I, P}; none connects to K or to G/X directly except via Level 3/4.
- From Level 1 {C, O}; neither connects to K.

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

- Target nodes are M (left) and B (center). List all neighbors and main routes from M to B.
- From M, there are only two immediate ways forward:
  1) M → E (top horizontal edge).
  2) M → J (left vertical edge downward).

Any path from M to B must start through E or through J.

Check both branches:

A) Through E:
- E connects to L (up), F (right), and B (down).
- From E there is a direct edge E → B. Therefore, if E remains, M–E–B is a valid path.

B) Through J:
- J connects to M (back), P (right), and D (down). From J → P → I → (to C, to right frame, etc.) and there are routes ultimately reaching B via:
  - I → C → N → B
  - I → P → O → C → N → B (same funnel)
  - Also J branch can go around outer rectangle to H → B, but that outer route still passes through I (via bottom edge J–P–I–…–H–B).
Hence, all J-originating paths to B must pass through I or P; however P connects to I, and from there to the rest. So removing I breaks every J→…→B route.

Minimal cut:
- To block the E branch, we must remove E (since E–B is direct; removing L or F wouldn’t stop E→B).
- To block the J branch, remove I (as argued, all J-based paths pass through I).

Verify sufficiency:
- Remove {E, I}.
- E removed eliminates M→E→B and any route via E→F…→H→B since E is the only connection from M to the right side along the top.
- I removed blocks all routes starting M→J→… because J’s only exit towards B ends up at I before reaching N/C/H/B.
- With both removed, no path from M to B remains.

Verify minimality:
- A single node cannot disconnect because:
  - Removing only E leaves paths M→J→P→I→C/N→B or around to H→B.
  - Removing only I leaves path M→E→B.
Therefore at least two nodes are needed, and {E, I} achieves disconnection.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, I]
--- Ground Truth ---

Model: gpt-5

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.

Targets: B (left) and M (top).

List the edges from M: (M,L), (M,E), (M,F), (M,K), and a long edge (M,P).

Any path from B to M must enter M through one of these five edges. If we remove all five, M is isolated, guaranteeing disconnection.

Verify smaller cuts are insufficient:
- If any one of those edges remains, there exists a path from B to that neighbor: 
  - To L: B–E–L (since B–E and E–L exist).
  - To E: B–E directly.
  - To F: B–E–F or B–H–G–F, etc.
  - To K: B–H–G–K or B–E–K, etc.
  - To P: B–P directly (via B–P edge).
Thus leaving any single edge allows a path to M, so at least 5 edges are necessary.

Therefore, the minimal cut is removing all edges incident to M.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 5, Cut Set: [(M, L), (M, E), (M, F), (M, K), (M, P)]
